### Question 116: Endemic Cretinism vs Dyshormonogenesis: Severe Iodine Deficiency
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the pathophysiology of endemic cretinism caused by severe iodine deficiency, its impact on thyroid hormone synthesis, and the most effective preventative strategy during critical developmental windows (pregnancy and early infancy). It differentiates this from other causes of congenital hypothyroidism like dyshormonogenesis or maternal antibodies.

#### Clinical Vignette:
In a remote, mountainous region characterized by iodine-deficient soil and water, and lacking salt iodization programs, a significant number of children are born with severe developmental delays. These children typically present with short stature, coarse facial features, umbilical hernias, delayed motor milestones, and profound intellectual disability (IQ < 70). Physical examination reveals massive, multinodular goiters. Laboratory investigations in affected individuals consistently show undetectable urinary iodine excretion (< 5 mcg/dL), markedly elevated serum TSH levels (often > 100 mIU/L), and severely low serum free T4 and free T3 concentrations (often < 1 ng/dL). Maternal thyroid function tests during pregnancy in this region also show elevated TSH and low free T4, indicating maternal hypothyroidism. Which of the following interventions is most crucial for preventing the severe neurodevelopmental sequelae (cretinism) associated with this condition when implemented during maternal pregnancy and early neonatal life?

(A) Universal dietary salt iodization and maternal potassium iodide supplementation
(B) High-dose oral calcium and vitamin D supplementation
(C) Prophylactic administration of levothyroxine to all pregnant women regardless of thyroid status
(D) Avoidance of dietary cruciferous vegetables containing goitrogens during the third trimester
(E) Routine neonatal screening with intramuscular injection of recombinant TSH

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Setting:** Isolated mountainous region with iodine-deficient soil/water, no salt iodization. This immediately points towards iodine deficiency as the primary etiology.
- **Patient Population:** Children born in this region.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Short stature, coarse facial features, umbilical hernias, severe mental retardation, massive goiters. This constellation of symptoms is classic for endemic cretinism.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Undetectable urinary iodine, markedly elevated TSH, severely reduced free T4 and T3. These findings confirm severe iodine deficiency leading to hypothyroidism. The high TSH indicates the pituitary is attempting to stimulate the thyroid, but the thyroid cannot produce sufficient hormone due to lack of substrate (iodine).
- **Maternal Findings:** Elevated TSH and low free T4 during pregnancy. This indicates maternal hypothyroidism due to iodine deficiency, which is critical for fetal neurodevelopment.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most effective* intervention to *completely prevent* the neurodevelopmental condition (cretinism) during maternal pregnancy and early neonatal life.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (甲状腺生理学与病理生理学分子机制推导)
- **Iodine Deficiency Pathophysiology:** Iodine is essential for the synthesis of thyroid hormones (T4 and T3). The thyroid follicular cell actively concentrates iodide from the circulation via the Sodium-Iodide Symporter (NIS, SLC5A5) located on the basolateral membrane. Iodide is then transported to the apical membrane via the Pendrin chloride-iodide exchanger (SLC26A4). Inside the follicular cell, thyroid peroxidase (TPO) oxidizes iodide to iodine and incorporates it onto tyrosine residues within thyroglobulin (Tg) to form monoiodotyrosine (MIT) and diiodotyrosine (DIT). Coupling of MIT and DIT forms T3, and coupling of two DIT molecules forms T4.
- **Severe Iodine Deficiency:** In severe deficiency, the thyroid cannot synthesize adequate T4 and T3. The lack of hormone leads to a loss of negative feedback on the hypothalamus and pituitary. TRH secretion increases, stimulating TSH release. TSH stimulates thyroid growth (goiter formation) and attempts to increase hormone synthesis, but without sufficient iodine, this is futile.
- **Endemic Cretinism:** This occurs when severe maternal iodine deficiency during pregnancy leads to fetal hypothyroidism. Thyroid hormones are crucial for fetal brain development, particularly during the second and third trimesters. Lack of T4/T3 results in irreversible neuronal damage, leading to severe intellectual disability, stunted growth, and other neurological deficits.
- **Prevention:** The most direct and effective way to prevent iodine deficiency disorders, including cretinism, is to ensure adequate iodine intake. This is achieved through universal salt iodization (adding potassium iodide to table salt) and, in severely deficient areas or during pregnancy, direct supplementation with potassium iodide.
- **Other Options Analysis:**
    - (B) Calcium and Vitamin D are important for bone health but do not address the underlying cause of cretinism (iodine deficiency).
    - (C) Levothyroxine treats hypothyroidism but does not address the root cause (lack of iodine substrate). While necessary for treating established hypothyroidism, it doesn't prevent the deficiency itself. Furthermore, universal screening and treatment of *all* pregnant women is less targeted and potentially less cost-effective than addressing the population-wide deficiency.
    - (D) Goitrogens (like those in cruciferous vegetables) can interfere with thyroid hormone synthesis, but their avoidance is relevant in individuals with borderline iodine status or pre-existing thyroid disease, not the primary prevention strategy for severe endemic iodine deficiency.
    - (E) Neonatal screening is crucial for detecting congenital hypothyroidism (including dyshormonogenesis), but it is a *detection* strategy, not a primary *prevention* strategy for iodine deficiency cretinism. Recombinant TSH is used for diagnosis, not treatment. Early treatment with levothyroxine is essential *after* detection, but preventing the deficiency itself is the most effective approach.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. Identify the clinical syndrome: The description (short stature, mental retardation, goiter, coarse features, umbilical hernia) in an iodine-deficient region points strongly to endemic cretinism.
2. Confirm the underlying cause: Laboratory findings (low urinary iodine, high TSH, low free T4/T3) confirm severe iodine deficiency leading to hypothyroidism.
3. Understand the pathophysiology: Iodine is required for thyroid hormone synthesis. Deficiency leads to inadequate hormone production, loss of negative feedback, pituitary stimulation (high TSH), goiter, and severe consequences for fetal brain development if it occurs during pregnancy.
4. Evaluate the intervention options:
    - (A) Addresses the root cause (iodine deficiency) directly and effectively prevents the condition during the critical period of pregnancy and early infancy.
    - (B) Irrelevant to iodine metabolism or thyroid function.
    - (C) Treats the consequence (hypothyroidism) but not the cause (iodine deficiency). Less effective for primary prevention in a population.
    - (D) Addresses a minor contributing factor (goitrogens) but not the primary cause.
    - (E) Is a diagnostic/treatment strategy *after* the condition develops, not a primary prevention strategy.
5. Select the most effective primary prevention strategy: Universal salt iodization and maternal supplementation directly provide the missing substrate (iodine), preventing the deficiency and its consequences.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Universal dietary salt iodization and maternal potassium iodide supplementation:** This is the cornerstone of preventing iodine deficiency disorders worldwide. Providing adequate iodine ensures the thyroid can synthesize sufficient hormone, preventing maternal and fetal hypothyroidism and subsequent cretinism. This directly addresses the root cause. **(Correct)**
- **(B) High-dose oral calcium and vitamin D supplementation:** Calcium and vitamin D are essential for bone health and calcium homeostasis. While severe hypothyroidism can affect bone metabolism, supplementation with calcium and vitamin D does not address the primary defect in thyroid hormone synthesis caused by iodine deficiency. This is incorrect.
- **(C) Prophylactic administration of levothyroxine to all pregnant women regardless of thyroid status:** Levothyroxine replaces thyroid hormone. While treating maternal hypothyroidism is crucial for fetal development, this option doesn't address the underlying iodine deficiency in the population. It treats the symptom (low hormone) but not the cause (lack of substrate). Furthermore, universal treatment without screening is less efficient than population-wide iodine supplementation. This is incorrect as a primary prevention strategy for iodine deficiency.
- **(D) Avoidance of dietary cruciferous vegetables containing goitrogens during the third trimester:** Goitrogens (e.g., glucosinolates in cruciferous vegetables) can interfere with thyroid hormone synthesis or uptake. However, in areas of severe iodine deficiency, the primary problem is the absolute lack of iodine, not the presence of goitrogens. Avoiding goitrogens might have a minor effect in borderline cases but is not the primary or most effective intervention for preventing endemic cretinism caused by severe deficiency. This is incorrect.
- **(E) Routine neonatal screening with intramuscular injection of recombinant TSH:** Neonatal screening (typically measuring TSH and/or T4) is essential for detecting congenital hypothyroidism, including cases caused by iodine deficiency or dyshormonogenesis. Early diagnosis allows for prompt treatment with levothyroxine, minimizing neurodevelopmental damage. However, this is a *secondary* prevention strategy (early detection and treatment) rather than a *primary* prevention strategy (preventing the deficiency itself). Recombinant TSH is used diagnostically, not therapeutically. This is incorrect as the *most effective* preventative measure.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Intervention                                      | Mechanism                                                                                                | Effectiveness for Iodine Deficiency Cretinism | Timing                                   |
| :------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- |
| Salt Iodization / KI Supplementation             | Provides essential iodine substrate for thyroid hormone synthesis.                                         | Highly effective (Primary Prevention)      | Maternal pregnancy & early infancy       |
| Levothyroxine Prophylaxis                         | Replaces missing thyroid hormone.                                                                        | Effective for treatment, less so for primary prevention in population | Maternal pregnancy                       |
| Calcium/Vitamin D Supplementation                | Supports bone health.                                                                                     | Ineffective for cretinism                  | N/A                                      |
| Goitrogen Avoidance                               | Reduces interference with thyroid hormone synthesis/uptake.                                                | Minor effect, not primary prevention       | Maternal pregnancy                       |
| Neonatal Screening & Levothyroxine Treatment     | Detects and treats congenital hypothyroidism early.                                                          | Effective for minimizing damage (Secondary Prevention) | Postnatal                                |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Severe iodine deficiency during pregnancy is the primary cause of endemic cretinism. The most effective strategy to prevent this devastating condition is ensuring adequate iodine intake through universal salt iodization and maternal supplementation, thereby providing the necessary substrate for thyroid hormone synthesis and preventing fetal hypothyroidism. This is a primary prevention measure targeting the root cause.

---

### Question 117: Lithium Pharmacotoxicity: Inhibition of Colloid Hydrolysis and Thyroid Hormone Release
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Lithium's effect on thyroid hormone synthesis and release, specifically its impact on colloid hydrolysis and hormone exocytosis (锂对甲状腺激素合成和释放的影响，特别是对胶体水解和激素胞吐作用的影响).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 36-year-old woman with bipolar I disorder has been maintained on lithium carbonate for 3 years with excellent mood stability. Over the past 4 months, she has developed cold intolerance, a 6-kg weight gain, dry skin, and a diffuse, non-tender goiter. Laboratory testing reveals:
- Serum TSH: 18.5 uIU/mL (normal: 0.5-4.5)
- Serum Free T4: 0.6 ng/dL (normal: 0.8-1.8)
- Anti-TPO Antibodies: Negative

Which of the following is the primary cellular mechanism by which lithium carbonate induces goiter and hypothyroidism?

(A) Inhibition of thyroid follicular lysosomal proteolysis and exocytosis, blocking the release of preformed T4 and T3 into the circulation
(B) Competitive blockade of the sodium-iodide symporter (NIS) preventing iodide entry
(C) Direct irreversible alkylation of the thyroid peroxidase catalytic heme group
(D) Transcriptional silencing of the thyroglobulin gene in the endoplasmic reticulum
(E) Accelerated peripheral deiodination of T4 to inactive reverse T3 by D3

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 36-year-old woman with bipolar disorder on lithium for 3 years. This immediately flags lithium as a potential cause of her symptoms.
- **Symptoms:** Cold intolerance, weight gain, dry skin, diffuse non-tender goiter. These are classic symptoms of hypothyroidism.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Elevated TSH (18.5 uIU/mL) and low Free T4 (0.6 ng/dL). This confirms primary hypothyroidism (high TSH due to lack of negative feedback from low T4).
- **Negative Anti-TPO Antibodies:** Rules out Hashimoto's thyroiditis as the primary cause, although lithium can sometimes exacerbate underlying autoimmune thyroid disease.
- **The Question:** Asks for the *primary cellular mechanism* by which lithium causes goiter and hypothyroidism.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (甲状腺生理学与病理生理学分子机制推导)
- **Lithium and Thyroid Function:** Lithium is known to interfere with thyroid hormone synthesis and release. It is a monovalent cation similar in size to sodium (Na+) and hydrogen (H+).
- **Mechanism of Action:** Lithium enters thyroid follicular cells via the Na+/K+ ATPase pump (similar to Na+). Once inside, it inhibits several steps in thyroid hormone production:
    - **Inhibition of Iodide Trapping:** Lithium can compete with sodium at the basolateral Na+/I- symporter (NIS), reducing iodide uptake into the follicular cell. However, this is generally considered a less significant effect compared to its impact on hormone release.
    - **Inhibition of Organification:** Lithium can inhibit thyroid peroxidase (TPO) activity, reducing the iodination of tyrosine residues on thyroglobulin (MIT and DIT formation).
    - **Inhibition of Hormone Release:** This is considered the *primary* mechanism. Lithium interferes with the proteolytic cleavage of thyroglobulin (Tg) within lysosomes and the subsequent exocytosis of T4 and T3 from the follicular cell into the circulation. It is thought to inhibit the activity of cathepsins (lysosomal proteases) involved in Tg breakdown and potentially interfere with the exocytotic process itself. This leads to an accumulation of colloid (Tg) within the follicles, causing goiter. The reduced release of T4 and T3 leads to hypothyroidism, which stimulates TSH secretion from the pituitary, further exacerbating the goiter.
- **Evaluating the Options:**
    - **(A) Inhibition of lysosomal proteolysis and exocytosis:** This aligns directly with the primary mechanism described above – lithium impairs the release of preformed hormones stored within the colloid.
    - **(B) Competitive blockade of NIS:** While lithium can affect NIS, it's not considered the primary mechanism for the significant hypothyroidism and goiter seen clinically.
    - **(C) Direct irreversible alkylation of TPO:** This mechanism is characteristic of thionamide drugs (PTU, methimazole), not lithium. Lithium inhibits TPO activity but not via irreversible alkylation.
    - **(D) Transcriptional silencing of the thyroglobulin gene:** Lithium does not directly affect Tg gene transcription.
    - **(E) Accelerated peripheral deiodination of T4 to rT3 by D3:** Lithium does not primarily affect peripheral deiodinase activity. Deiodinases (D1, D2, D3) are selenocysteine-containing enzymes involved in converting T4 to T3 or inactivating T4/T3.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient presents with symptoms (cold intolerance, weight gain, dry skin, goiter) and lab findings (high TSH, low free T4) consistent with primary hypothyroidism.
2. The patient has a history of bipolar disorder and is taking lithium.
3. Lithium is a known cause of hypothyroidism and goiter.
4. The question asks for the primary mechanism of lithium-induced thyroid dysfunction.
5. Review the known mechanisms of lithium's effect on the thyroid: inhibition of iodide trapping, inhibition of organification, inhibition of hormone release (proteolysis/exocytosis).
6. The inhibition of hormone release (proteolysis and exocytosis) is generally considered the most significant mechanism leading to goiter (colloid accumulation) and hypothyroidism (reduced hormone levels).
7. Evaluate the options based on this understanding. Option (A) accurately describes the primary mechanism. Options (B), (C), (D), and (E) describe incorrect or less significant mechanisms.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Inhibition of thyroid follicular lysosomal proteolysis and exocytosis, blocking the release of preformed T4 and T3 into the circulation:** This is the correct mechanism. Lithium accumulates in thyroid follicular cells and interferes with the final steps of hormone release, leading to colloid accumulation (goiter) and reduced circulating hormone levels (hypothyroidism).
- **(B) Competitive blockade of the sodium-iodide symporter (NIS) preventing iodide entry:** Lithium can compete with Na+ at the NIS, but this effect is relatively minor compared to its impact on hormone release. It doesn't fully explain the significant hypothyroidism and goiter typically seen.
- **(C) Direct irreversible alkylation of the thyroid peroxidase catalytic heme group:** This is the mechanism of action for thionamide drugs (PTU, methimazole), which inhibit TPO-mediated organification. Lithium inhibits TPO activity but not through irreversible alkylation.
- **(D) Transcriptional silencing of the thyroglobulin gene in the endoplasmic reticulum:** Lithium does not affect Tg gene transcription. Tg is synthesized and stored in the colloid lumen.
- **(E) Accelerated peripheral deiodination of T4 to inactive reverse T3 by D3:** Lithium does not primarily affect peripheral deiodinase activity. While severe illness can alter deiodinase activity (e.g., increased D3 activity leading to high rT3), this is not the mechanism of lithium-induced hypothyroidism.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Mechanism                     | Lithium Effect                                   | Thionamides (PTU/MMI) | Other Notes                                                                 |
| :---------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------- | :------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Iodide Trapping (NIS)         | Mild inhibition (competes with Na+)              | None                | Perchlorate, pertechnetate also inhibit NIS.                               |
| Organification (TPO)          | Inhibition (non-alkylating)                      | Irreversible alkylation | PTU also inhibits peripheral T4->T3 conversion.                             |
| Colloid Hydrolysis/Exocytosis | **Primary effect: Inhibition**                    | None                | Leads to colloid accumulation (goiter) and reduced hormone release.         |
| Peripheral Deiodination       | No significant effect                             | PTU inhibits D1      | D1 inhibition by PTU increases plasma T3.                                   |
| Gene Transcription (Tg)       | No effect                                        | None                |                                                                             |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Lithium carbonate, used to treat bipolar disorder, is a well-known cause of hypothyroidism and goiter. Its primary mechanism involves inhibiting the release of preformed thyroid hormones (T4 and T3) from the thyroid follicular cell by interfering with lysosomal proteolysis of thyroglobulin and subsequent exocytosis. This leads to accumulation of colloid within the follicles (goiter) and decreased circulating thyroid hormone levels (hypothyroidism), triggering a compensatory rise in TSH. While lithium can also mildly inhibit iodide trapping and organification, the inhibition of hormone release is considered the most clinically significant effect.

### Question 118: Immune Checkpoint Inhibitor-Induced Endocrine Dysfunction
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Immune checkpoint inhibitors (ICIs) like anti-PD-1 antibodies can cause immune-related adverse events (irAEs), including thyroid dysfunction, by releasing immune tolerance and allowing autoreactive T cells to attack endocrine tissues. This often manifests as a biphasic pattern of hyperthyroidism followed by hypothyroidism, characteristic of destructive thyroiditis.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old man with metastatic melanoma is being treated with pembrolizumab, an anti-PD-1 monoclonal antibody. Six weeks into treatment, his TSH is suppressed (<0.01 uIU/mL) and free T4 is elevated, indicating hyperthyroidism. Four weeks later (10 weeks after starting pembrolizumab), despite not receiving antithyroid medication, he develops symptoms of fatigue and weight gain. Repeat laboratory tests show a markedly elevated TSH (48 uIU/mL) and a low free T4 (0.4 ng/dL), consistent with primary hypothyroidism. What is the most likely underlying mechanism responsible for this sequence of events?

(A) Disinhibition of autoreactive cytotoxic T cells leading to destructive autoimmune thyroiditis followed by permanent primary hypothyroidism
(B) Direct pembrolizumab-mediated activation of the TSH receptor causing transient hyperthyroidism
(C) Pembrolizumab-induced hypothalamic necrosis resulting in central panhypopituitarism
(D) Formation of anti-thyroxine neutralizing antibodies that accelerate hormone degradation
(E) Severe secondary amyloidosis localized exclusively to the thyroid gland

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old man with metastatic melanoma. This indicates a serious underlying malignancy requiring systemic therapy.
- **Treatment:** Pembrolizumab (anti-PD-1 antibody). This is a key piece of information, linking the thyroid events to immunotherapy. PD-1 is an immune checkpoint receptor; blocking it enhances T cell activity.
- **Timeline & Lab Findings:**
    - 6 weeks post-pembrolizumab: Suppressed TSH (<0.01 uIU/mL), elevated free T4. This is hyperthyroidism.
    - 10 weeks post-pembrolizumab (4 weeks after the hyperthyroid phase): Symptoms of fatigue and weight gain, elevated TSH (48 uIU/mL), low free T4 (0.4 ng/dL). This is primary hypothyroidism.
- **Absence of Antithyroid Drugs:** This rules out drug-induced hypothyroidism as the cause of the second phase.
- **Biphasic Pattern:** The sequence of hyperthyroidism followed by hypothyroidism is highly characteristic of destructive thyroiditis.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (甲状腺生理学与病理生理学分子机制推导)
- **Immune Checkpoint Inhibitors (ICIs):** Drugs like pembrolizumab block inhibitory signals (PD-1/PD-L1) on T cells, unleashing the immune system to attack cancer cells. However, this can also lead to the breakdown of self-tolerance, causing the immune system to attack normal tissues, resulting in irAEs.
- **Thyroid irAEs:** The thyroid gland is a common target for irAEs. The mechanism involves the activation of autoreactive T cells (specifically cytotoxic T lymphocytes, CTLs) that infiltrate the thyroid gland.
- **Destructive Thyroiditis:** These activated CTLs attack and destroy thyroid follicular cells.
    - **Hyperthyroid Phase:** Initial destruction releases pre-formed thyroid hormones (T4 and T3) stored in the colloid, leading to transient hyperthyroidism. The pituitary gland senses the high levels of T4/T3 and suppresses TSH secretion.
    - **Hypothyroid Phase:** As the follicular cells are destroyed, the gland's capacity to synthesize and release new hormones diminishes. Eventually, the stored hormone is depleted, and the damaged gland cannot produce sufficient T4/T3. The pituitary gland, no longer suppressed by high T4/T3, increases TSH secretion in an attempt to stimulate the failing thyroid, resulting in primary hypothyroidism.
- **Pembrolizumab Mechanism:** Pembrolizumab binds to PD-1 on T cells, preventing its interaction with PD-L1 on tumor cells and antigen-presenting cells. This blockade removes the inhibitory signal, allowing T cells (including autoreactive ones) to become fully activated and exert their cytotoxic effects.
- **Differential Diagnosis:**
    - Graves' disease (autoimmune hyperthyroidism) is mediated by TSH receptor antibodies (TRAb/TSI) that stimulate the TSH receptor, causing sustained hyperthyroidism, often with goiter and ophthalmopathy. The biphasic pattern and eventual hypothyroidism are not typical.
    - Hashimoto's thyroiditis (autoimmune hypothyroidism) involves antibodies against TPO and Tg and lymphocytic infiltration, leading primarily to hypothyroidism, although a transient hyperthyroid phase (hashitoxicosis) can occur early on. However, the rapid sequence triggered by ICI therapy points more strongly to ICI-induced destructive thyroiditis.
    - Central hypothyroidism would involve pituitary or hypothalamic dysfunction, leading to low TSH and low free T4. This patient has high TSH, indicating primary thyroid failure.
    - Drug-induced hypothyroidism (e.g., amiodarone, lithium) or hyperthyroidism (e.g., iodine-induced Jod-Basedow) have different mechanisms and are not suggested by the clinical context.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  **Identify the key intervention:** Pembrolizumab, an anti-PD-1 ICI.
2.  **Recognize the known side effects:** ICIs are associated with irAEs, including thyroid dysfunction.
3.  **Analyze the thyroid function tests:** The patient initially presents with hyperthyroidism (low TSH, high free T4) followed by hypothyroidism (high TSH, low free T4) after starting pembrolizumab.
4.  **Interpret the biphasic pattern:** The sequence of hyperthyroidism followed by hypothyroidism is characteristic of thyroid tissue destruction, where stored hormone is released first, followed by impaired synthesis.
5.  **Connect the mechanism to the drug:** ICIs like pembrolizumab remove immune checkpoints, potentially activating autoreactive T cells that target the thyroid gland, causing destructive thyroiditis.
6.  **Evaluate the options:**
    - (A) aligns perfectly with the known mechanism of ICI-induced thyroiditis (disinhibition of autoreactive T cells -> destructive thyroiditis -> biphasic thyroid dysfunction -> primary hypothyroidism).
    - (B) is incorrect; pembrolizumab does not directly activate the TSH receptor.
    - (C) is incorrect; the high TSH indicates primary thyroid failure, not central hypothyroidism.
    - (D) is incorrect; while antibodies can be involved in thyroid disease, the primary mechanism here is T-cell mediated destruction, and neutralizing anti-T4 antibodies are not the cause.
    - (E) is incorrect; amyloidosis is not a recognized mechanism for ICI-induced thyroid dysfunction.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Disinhibition of autoreactive cytotoxic T cells leading to destructive autoimmune thyroiditis followed by permanent primary hypothyroidism:** This is the correct mechanism. ICIs unleash autoreactive T cells, which attack the thyroid, causing inflammation and destruction (thyroiditis). The initial destruction leads to hormone release (hyperthyroidism), followed by gland failure (hypothyroidism). While often transient, it can become permanent. This aligns with the clinical presentation.
- **(B) Direct pembrolizumab-mediated activation of the TSH receptor causing transient hyperthyroidism:** Pembrolizumab is an antibody targeting PD-1 on T cells. It does not directly interact with or activate the TSH receptor on thyroid follicular cells. This mechanism is incorrect.
- **(C) Pembrolizumab-induced hypothalamic necrosis resulting in central panhypopituitarism:** Hypothalamic necrosis is a rare but possible severe irAE. However, it would cause central hypothyroidism (low TSH, low free T4) and potentially deficiencies of other pituitary hormones. The patient has high TSH, indicating primary thyroid failure, ruling this out.
- **(D) Formation of anti-thyroxine neutralizing antibodies that accelerate hormone degradation:** Neutralizing antibodies against T4 would lead to decreased levels of active hormone, potentially causing hypothyroidism. However, this mechanism does not explain the initial hyperthyroid phase caused by hormone release from damaged cells. Furthermore, this is not the established mechanism for ICI-induced thyroiditis.
- **(E) Severe secondary amyloidosis localized exclusively to the thyroid gland:** Amyloidosis can affect the thyroid, potentially causing hypothyroidism or goiter, but it is not a known complication of ICI therapy and does not explain the biphasic pattern of hyperthyroidism followed by hypothyroidism.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Feature                 | ICI-Induced Thyroiditis                                  | Graves' Disease                                        | Hashimoto's Thyroiditis                               |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------ | :----------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------- |
| **Mechanism**           | T-cell mediated destruction of thyroid follicular cells | TSH receptor stimulating antibodies (TRAb/TSI)         | Autoantibodies (anti-TPO, anti-Tg), T-cell infiltration |
| **TSH**                 | Initially suppressed (hyperthyroid), then elevated (hypo) | Suppressed                                             | Often normal or elevated (hypothyroid)                  |
| **Free T4**             | Initially elevated (hyperthyroid), then low (hypo)       | Elevated                                               | Low (hypothyroid)                                      |
| **RAIU**                | Typically low (destructive process)                      | High (stimulated gland)                                | Normal or low                                          |
| **Antibodies**          | Often negative or transiently positive for TPO/Tg Ab      | Positive for TRAb/TSI                                  | Positive for anti-TPO, anti-Tg                          |
| **Clinical Course**     | Biphasic (hyper -> hypo)                                | Sustained hyperthyroidism                               | Progressive hypothyroidism                              |
| **Association**         | Immune checkpoint inhibitors (e.g., pembrolizumab)      | Autoimmune disease                                     | Autoimmune disease                                     |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Immune checkpoint inhibitors (ICIs) like anti-PD-1 antibodies can cause immune-related adverse events (irAEs) by releasing immune tolerance. Thyroid dysfunction is a common irAE, often presenting as a biphasic pattern of transient hyperthyroidism followed by hypothyroidism, resulting from T-cell mediated destruction of thyroid follicular cells (destructive thyroiditis). This mechanism highlights the importance of understanding both the therapeutic effects and potential immune-mediated toxicities of cancer immunotherapy.

---

### Question 119: Thyroid Storm: Clinical Presentation, Diagnostic Criteria, and Multi-Organ Failure
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Thyroid storm is a life-threatening exacerbation of hyperthyroidism, often precipitated by acute stressors in patients with underlying Graves' disease or toxic multinodular goiter. Understanding the triggers and pathophysiology is crucial. (甲状腺风暴是甲亢的危及生命的恶化，通常由潜在的 Graves 病或毒性多结节性甲状腺肿患者中的急性应激因素引发。理解触发因素和病理生理学至关重要。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 26-year-old woman with a known history of poorly controlled Graves' disease presents to the emergency department after a motor vehicle collision. She is described as profoundly agitated, combative, and delirious. Her vital signs are: temperature 40.2°C (104.4°F), heart rate 165 bpm (irregularly irregular, consistent with atrial fibrillation), blood pressure 160/70 mmHg, and respiratory rate 28/min. Physical examination reveals significant diaphoresis, severe exophthalmos, a large goiter with a palpable thrill, mild scleral icterus, and bilateral lung crackles with peripheral edema. Laboratory results show elevated serum free T4 (>10 ng/dL) and free T3 (>20 pg/mL), suppressed TSH (<0.01 µIU/mL), elevated liver enzymes (AST 180 U/L, ALT 210 U/L), elevated creatine kinase (CK 1500 U/L), and leukocytosis (WBC 18,000/µL). The Burch-Wartofsky Point Scale score is calculated as 75, strongly indicating thyroid storm. Which of the following is the most common precipitating factor for thyroid storm in a patient with underlying thyrotoxicosis?

(A) Acute systemic stressor (e.g., infection, trauma, surgery, or non-compliance with antithyroid medications)
(B) Sudden dietary restriction of sodium and iodine
(C) Ingestion of high-dose acetaminophen for tension headache
(D) Acute discontinuation of levothyroxine therapy
(E) Excessive intake of dietary goitrogens such as cassava or cabbage

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 26-year-old woman with poorly controlled Graves' disease. This establishes the underlying condition (hyperthyroidism due to Graves') and suggests a predisposition to thyroid storm.
- **Presenting Event:** Motor vehicle collision (trauma). This is a significant acute stressor.
- **Symptoms:** Agitation, combativeness, delirium (CNS manifestations of severe hyperthyroidism).
- **Vital Signs:** High fever (40.2°C), tachycardia (165 bpm, atrial fibrillation), hypertension (160/70 mmHg), tachypnea (28/min). These indicate systemic hypermetabolism and cardiovascular compromise.
- **Physical Exam:** Diaphoresis (increased sweating), severe exophthalmos (Graves' ophthalmopathy), large goiter with thrill (hyperactive thyroid gland), scleral icterus (liver dysfunction), lung crackles and peripheral edema (heart failure/fluid overload). These point to multi-organ involvement characteristic of thyroid storm.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Markedly elevated free T4 and free T3, suppressed TSH (confirming severe hyperthyroidism), elevated liver enzymes (hepatotoxicity), elevated CK (rhabdomyolysis/myopathy), leukocytosis (stress response/infection).
- **Burch-Wartofsky Score:** 75 (confirms diagnosis of thyroid storm).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most common* precipitating factor for thyroid storm.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (甲状腺生理学与病理生理学分子机制推导)
- **Thyroid Storm Pathophysiology:** Thyroid storm represents an acute, severe exacerbation of hyperthyroidism. Excess thyroid hormones (T4 and T3) amplify the basal metabolic rate and increase the sensitivity of tissues to catecholamines, leading to a hypermetabolic state affecting multiple organ systems (cardiovascular, neurological, hepatic, gastrointestinal, hematologic). The underlying mechanism involves increased synthesis and release of thyroid hormones, often coupled with increased peripheral conversion of T4 to T3 (mediated by Type 1 deiodinase, D1).
- **Precipitating Factors:** While the exact trigger can vary, thyroid storm is almost always precipitated by an acute stressor in individuals with pre-existing, often poorly controlled, hyperthyroidism. Common stressors include:
    - **Infection:** Systemic inflammation increases metabolic demands and can stimulate thyroid hormone release.
    - **Trauma:** Physical injury, like the motor vehicle collision in this case, triggers a stress response.
    - **Surgery:** Surgical stress is a well-known trigger.
    - **Non-compliance with antithyroid medications:** Abrupt cessation or inadequate dosing allows thyroid hormone levels to rise unchecked.
    - **Radioactive iodine therapy:** Can cause a transient release of stored hormone (radiation thyroiditis).
    - **Parturition:** Postpartum thyroiditis can sometimes trigger storm.
    - **Certain medications:** Amiodarone, iodine administration (Jod-Basedow effect in autonomous nodules, but less common in Graves').
- **Why other options are less likely:**
    - **(B) Sudden dietary restriction of sodium and iodine:** While iodine deficiency can cause hypothyroidism or goiter, severe restriction is unlikely to *precipitate* a storm in someone already hyperthyroid. In fact, high iodine loads can sometimes worsen hyperthyroidism (Jod-Basedow), but restriction is not a typical trigger for storm.
    - **(C) Ingestion of high-dose acetaminophen:** Acetaminophen is primarily hepatotoxic in overdose. While liver dysfunction is seen in thyroid storm, acetaminophen itself is not a recognized trigger.
    - **(D) Acute discontinuation of levothyroxine therapy:** Levothyroxine is synthetic T4 used to treat *hypo*thyroidism. Discontinuing it would lead to hypothyroidism, not hyperthyroidism or thyroid storm. Patients with Graves' disease are treated with antithyroid drugs (PTU, methimazole), beta-blockers, radioactive iodine, or surgery, not levothyroxine.
    - **(E) Excessive intake of dietary goitrogens:** Goitrogens (e.g., in cassava, cabbage) interfere with thyroid hormone synthesis, typically leading to hypothyroidism or goiter, especially in iodine-deficient areas. They do not cause hyperthyroidism or precipitate thyroid storm.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient presents with classic signs and symptoms of thyroid storm (fever, tachycardia, agitation, delirium, multi-organ dysfunction) in the context of known Graves' disease.
2. The Burch-Wartofsky score confirms the diagnosis.
3. The question asks for the *most common* precipitating factor.
4. Review the patient's history: She has poorly controlled Graves' disease and recently experienced a motor vehicle collision (trauma).
5. Consider the known triggers for thyroid storm: Acute stressors are the most frequent culprits.
6. Evaluate the options:
    - (A) Acute systemic stressor (trauma fits this category) is a well-established and common trigger.
    - (B) Dietary restriction is not a typical trigger.
    - (C) Acetaminophen is unrelated.
    - (D) Levothyroxine is for hypothyroidism, irrelevant here.
    - (E) Goitrogens cause hypothyroidism, not storm.
7. Therefore, the acute systemic stressor (trauma) is the most likely and most common type of precipitating factor.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Acute systemic stressor (e.g., infection, trauma, surgery, or non-compliance with antithyroid medications):** This is the correct answer. Trauma (motor vehicle collision) is a classic acute stressor that can precipitate thyroid storm in susceptible individuals with underlying hyperthyroidism. Non-compliance is also a very common trigger. This option encompasses the most frequent causes.
- **(B) Sudden dietary restriction of sodium and iodine:** Incorrect. Iodine restriction is used to treat hyperthyroidism sometimes, but sudden restriction is not a trigger for storm. In fact, excessive iodine can sometimes worsen hyperthyroidism (Jod-Basedow phenomenon), but restriction is not a common precipitant. Sodium restriction is relevant for heart failure or hypertension, not thyroid storm precipitation.
- **(C) Ingestion of high-dose acetaminophen for tension headache:** Incorrect. Acetaminophen overdose causes hepatotoxicity. While liver dysfunction occurs in thyroid storm, acetaminophen itself is not a known precipitant. The patient's scleral icterus is likely due to the storm itself, not acetaminophen.
- **(D) Acute discontinuation of levothyroxine therapy:** Incorrect. Levothyroxine is used to treat hypothyroidism. Discontinuing it would worsen hypothyroidism, not cause hyperthyroidism or thyroid storm. This option confuses the treatment for hyperthyroidism (antithyroid drugs) with the treatment for hypothyroidism (levothyroxine).
- **(E) Excessive intake of dietary goitrogens such as cassava or cabbage:** Incorrect. Goitrogens interfere with thyroid hormone synthesis and typically cause or worsen hypothyroidism, especially in iodine-deficient individuals. They do not cause hyperthyroidism or precipitate thyroid storm.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Feature             | Thyroid Storm Precipitant                                  | Incorrect Options (B, C, D, E)                                  |
|----------------------|------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Mechanism**        | Acute stressor exacerbates underlying hyperthyroidism       | Dietary restriction, drug toxicity, inappropriate medication, goitrogens |
| **Common Causes**    | Infection, trauma, surgery, non-compliance, iodine excess | None of these are common triggers for storm.                     |
| **Patient Context**  | Pre-existing hyperthyroidism (e.g., Graves')              | Irrelevant or contradictory to hyperthyroidism/storm physiology. |
| **Physiological Effect**| Increased catecholamine sensitivity, hypermetabolism       | Hypothyroidism, hepatotoxicity, etc.                             |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Thyroid storm is a medical emergency triggered most commonly by acute stressors in patients with underlying hyperthyroidism. Recognizing these triggers (infection, trauma, surgery, medication non-compliance) is crucial for prevention and prompt management. The pathophysiology involves exaggerated thyroid hormone effects amplified by increased sensitivity to catecholamines.

---

### Question 120: Thyroid Storm Management: Immediate Non-Selective Beta-Blockade with Propranolol
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Management of thyroid storm, specifically the rationale for using non-selective beta-blockade (propranolol) over selective beta-1 blockade (metoprolol, atenolol, esmolol) due to its additional peripheral effect on T4 to T3 conversion via inhibition of Type 1 deiodinase (D1).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old woman with a known history of Graves' disease presents to the emergency department with fever (40°C), tachycardia (150 bpm), agitation, diaphoresis, and tremors. Her symptoms began acutely 2 days ago after discontinuing her prescribed methimazole. Physical examination reveals a diffusely enlarged, warm, and tender thyroid gland, bilateral exophthalmos, and fine tremors. Vital signs show a heart rate of 150 bpm, blood pressure of 160/100 mmHg, respiratory rate of 28 breaths/min, and temperature of 40°C. Laboratory results show suppressed TSH (<0.01 µIU/mL), markedly elevated free T4 (5.0 ng/dL; normal 0.8-1.8 ng/dL), and elevated free T3 (15 pg/mL; normal 2.3-4.2 pg/mL). The patient is diagnosed with thyroid storm. The emergency medicine team establishes intravenous access and initiates urgent multimodal therapy. Which of the following medications is administered immediately to control adrenergic hyperactivity and tachycardia, and why is propranolol specifically preferred over cardioselective beta-1 blockers?

(A) Propranolol; it rapidly antagonizes adrenergic receptors and at high doses uniquely inhibits peripheral Type 1 deiodinase (D1), reducing T4-to-T3 conversion
(B) Metoprolol; it selectively blocks beta-1 receptors without affecting peripheral vascular resistance or bronchial tone
(C) Atenolol; it is completely cleared by the kidneys, preventing hepatic drug accumulation
(D) Labetalol; its combined alpha-1 blockade causes immediate restoration of normal vascular tone
(E) Esmolol; it is administered exclusively as an oral bolus to prevent thyroid hormone release

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 45-year-old woman with Graves' disease (autoimmune hyperthyroidism).
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Acute onset of high fever, severe tachycardia, agitation, diaphoresis, tremors – classic signs of thyroid storm, a life-threatening exacerbation of hyperthyroidism.
- **Physical Exam:** Diffuse goiter (consistent with Graves'), exophthalmos (specific to Graves'), tremors (adrenergic excess).
- **Vital Signs:** Tachycardia (150 bpm), hypertension (160/100 mmHg), hyperthermia (40°C), tachypnea (28 breaths/min) – reflect systemic adrenergic overdrive and increased metabolic rate.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Suppressed TSH (<0.01), markedly elevated free T4 (5.0 ng/dL), elevated free T3 (15 pg/mL) – confirm severe hyperthyroidism.
- **Trigger:** Discontinuation of methimazole (antithyroid drug) – a common precipitant for thyroid storm in patients with poorly controlled Graves'.
- **Diagnosis:** Thyroid storm.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the immediate medication to control adrenergic symptoms (tachycardia, tremors, agitation) and the specific reason for choosing propranolol over selective beta-1 blockers.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (甲状腺生理学与病理生理学分子机制推导)
- **Thyroid Storm Pathophysiology:** Exaggerated hyperthyroidism leading to overwhelming sympathetic nervous system activation and increased tissue sensitivity to catecholamines. Excess thyroid hormone (T4 and T3) increases the number and sensitivity of beta-adrenergic receptors in various tissues (heart, vasculature, brain, skeletal muscle). This leads to tachycardia, palpitations, hypertension, tremor, anxiety, hyperthermia, and potentially cardiac failure or arrhythmias.
- **Role of Beta-Blockers:** Beta-blockers are crucial for managing the adrenergic symptoms of thyroid storm. They counteract the effects of increased catecholamines and heightened adrenergic receptor sensitivity.
- **Propranolol vs. Selective Beta-1 Blockers:**
    - **Propranolol:** A non-selective beta-blocker (blocks both beta-1 and beta-2 receptors). It also possesses intrinsic sympathomimetic activity (ISA) at beta-2 receptors, although this is less relevant in this context. Crucially, at high doses (typically used in thyroid storm), propranolol inhibits the enzyme Type 1 deiodinase (D1). Type 1 deiodinase is primarily located in the liver, kidney, and thyroid, and it converts peripheral T4 to the more active T3. By inhibiting D1, propranolol reduces the production of T3 from T4, thereby mitigating the overall hyperthyroid state.
    - **Selective Beta-1 Blockers (e.g., Metoprolol, Atenolol, Esmolol):** Primarily block beta-1 receptors located mainly in the heart. They are effective at reducing heart rate and contractility but do *not* significantly inhibit peripheral T4 to T3 conversion by D1. Blocking only beta-1 receptors might leave beta-2 mediated effects (like tremor, anxiety, peripheral vasodilation) relatively unaddressed, and importantly, fails to address the peripheral T3 overproduction contributing to the storm.
- **Why Propranolol is Preferred:** In thyroid storm, controlling both the adrenergic symptoms *and* reducing the production of the more potent T3 is essential. Propranolol achieves both goals. The inhibition of D1 is a unique property of propranolol (and some other non-selective beta-blockers like nadolol) at higher doses, making it the preferred agent in this specific emergency.
- **Other Options:**
    - **Metoprolol/Atenolol/Esmolol:** Lack the D1 inhibitory effect, making them less effective in addressing the underlying hormonal excess.
    - **Labetalol:** Blocks both alpha-1 and beta receptors. While it lowers blood pressure, it doesn't inhibit D1 and might cause excessive vasodilation. Alpha blockade is not the primary goal in thyroid storm management.
    - **Esmolol:** Ultra-short-acting beta-1 blocker, administered intravenously. Lacks D1 inhibition. Oral administration is incorrect for acute thyroid storm management.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  The patient presents with classic signs and symptoms of thyroid storm (fever, tachycardia, agitation, tremor, hypertension) confirmed by lab tests (suppressed TSH, high free T4, high free T3).
2.  The immediate goals of management are to control adrenergic symptoms (tachycardia, hypertension, tremor, agitation) and reduce thyroid hormone levels/effects.
3.  Beta-blockers are indicated to control adrenergic symptoms.
4.  The question asks why propranolol is preferred over selective beta-1 blockers.
5.  Propranolol is a non-selective beta-blocker. Non-selective beta-blockers, particularly propranolol at higher doses, inhibit Type 1 deiodinase (D1).
6.  D1 converts peripheral T4 to T3. Inhibiting D1 reduces the production of the more active T3 hormone.
7.  Selective beta-1 blockers do not inhibit D1.
8.  Therefore, propranolol is preferred because it addresses both the adrenergic symptoms (via beta-1 and beta-2 blockade) and the underlying hormonal excess (by inhibiting peripheral T4 to T3 conversion).

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Propranolol; it rapidly antagonizes adrenergic receptors and at high doses uniquely inhibits peripheral Type 1 deiodinase (D1), reducing T4-to-T3 conversion:** This is the correct answer. Propranolol blocks both beta-1 and beta-2 receptors, controlling adrenergic symptoms. Its unique ability to inhibit D1 at therapeutic doses used in thyroid storm helps reduce T3 levels, addressing the underlying hormonal cause.
- **(B) Metoprolol; it selectively blocks beta-1 receptors without affecting peripheral vascular resistance or bronchial tone:** Incorrect. Metoprolol is a selective beta-1 blocker. While it controls cardiac symptoms (tachycardia), it does not block beta-2 receptors (which contribute to tremor, anxiety) and, crucially, does not inhibit D1, failing to reduce peripheral T3 production. The statement about not affecting peripheral vascular resistance is also misleading, as beta-2 blockade can cause vasoconstriction.
- **(C) Atenolol; it is completely cleared by the kidneys, preventing hepatic drug accumulation:** Incorrect. Atenolol is a selective beta-1 blocker, similar limitations as metoprolol regarding D1 inhibition and beta-2 blockade. The statement about renal clearance is true but irrelevant to the primary reason for choosing propranolol over selective beta-blockers in thyroid storm. Furthermore, renal function might be compromised in severe illness.
- **(D) Labetalol; its combined alpha-1 blockade causes immediate restoration of normal vascular tone:** Incorrect. Labetalol is a combined alpha-1 and non-selective beta blocker. While it lowers blood pressure, alpha-1 blockade is not the primary therapeutic target in thyroid storm, and it lacks the specific D1 inhibitory effect of propranolol. Unopposed alpha blockade can sometimes worsen hypertension initially.
- **(E) Esmolol; it is administered exclusively as an oral bolus to prevent thyroid hormone release:** Incorrect. Esmolol is an ultra-short-acting selective beta-1 blocker administered intravenously. It lacks D1 inhibition. The statement about oral administration is incorrect; it's given IV. It also does not prevent thyroid hormone release.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Feature                 | Propranolol (Non-selective) | Metoprolol/Atenolol/Esmolol (Selective Beta-1) | Labetalol (Alpha+Beta) |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :-------------------- |
| Beta-1 Blockade         | Yes                         | Yes                                            | Yes                  |
| Beta-2 Blockade         | Yes                         | No                                             | Yes                  |
| Alpha-1 Blockade        | No                          | No                                             | Yes                  |
| D1 Inhibition (High Dose) | Yes                         | No                                             | No                   |
| Primary Use in Storm    | Yes                         | No (Less effective)                             | No                   |
| Administration          | IV/Oral                     | IV/Oral                                         | IV/Oral              |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
In thyroid storm, non-selective beta-blockers like propranolol are preferred over selective beta-1 blockers because they provide broader adrenergic blockade (beta-1 and beta-2) and uniquely inhibit peripheral Type 1 deiodinase (D1) at higher doses, reducing the conversion of T4 to the more active T3, thus addressing both the symptoms and the underlying hormonal pathophysiology.

---

### Question 121: Thyroid Storm Management: Propylthiouracil as the Preferred Thionamide
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Pharmacological differences between propylthiouracil (PTU) and methimazole (MMI) in the context of thyroid storm, specifically PTU's inhibition of peripheral T4 to T3 conversion by Type 1 deiodinase (D1).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old woman with a known history of Graves' disease presents to the emergency department with fever (40°C), tachycardia (150 bpm), agitation, and diaphoresis. Her symptoms began acutely 2 days ago after discontinuing her methimazole medication due to perceived side effects. Physical examination reveals a diffusely enlarged, warm, and tender thyroid gland, fine tremor, and mild exophthalmos. Vital signs show a blood pressure of 140/90 mmHg and respiratory rate of 28 breaths/min. Laboratory results show suppressed TSH (<0.01 µIU/mL), markedly elevated free T4 (8.0 ng/dL; normal 0.8-1.8 ng/dL), and elevated free T3 (25 pg/mL; normal 2.3-4.2 pg/mL). A Burch-Wartofsky score confirms thyroid storm. Initial management includes intravenous fluids, cooling measures, and propranolol (10 mg IV followed by oral dosing). Subsequently, an antithyroid medication is administered via nasogastric tube. Propylthiouracil (PTU, 500 mg loading dose followed by 200 mg every 4 hours) is chosen over methimazole. What is the primary pharmacologic reason for selecting PTU in this acute setting?

(A) PTU provides dual action by inhibiting thyroid peroxidase within the gland and inhibiting peripheral conversion of T4 to T3 by Type 1 deiodinase
(B) PTU has a much longer serum half-life than methimazole and requires once-daily dosing
(C) PTU binds directly to the TSH receptor and blocks thyroid-stimulating immunoglobulin binding
(D) PTU carries zero risk of hepatotoxicity compared to methimazole
(E) PTU accelerates the renal tubular excretion of preformed thyroid hormones

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 45-year-old woman with Graves' disease history, presenting with classic signs and symptoms of thyroid storm (fever, tachycardia, agitation, diaphoresis, tremor, exophthalmos, warm/tender goiter).
- **Laboratory Findings:** Suppressed TSH, markedly elevated free T4 and free T3, confirming severe hyperthyroidism.
- **Diagnosis:** Thyroid storm, a life-threatening exacerbation of hyperthyroidism.
- **Initial Management:** Standard measures initiated (fluids, cooling, beta-blockade).
- **Specific Question:** Why is PTU preferred over methimazole in this acute setting? This points to a specific pharmacological difference between the two thionamides.
- **Key Clue:** The question asks for the *pharmacologic advantage* of PTU *during an acute thyrotoxic crisis*.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (甲状腺生理学与病理生理学分子机制推导)
- **Thyroid Storm Pathophysiology:** Excessive release of thyroid hormones (T4 and T3) leading to systemic hypermetabolism and adrenergic overdrive. T3 is significantly more potent than T4.
- **Antithyroid Drugs (Thionamides):** PTU and methimazole inhibit thyroid hormone synthesis by blocking the enzyme thyroid peroxidase (TPO). TPO is crucial for iodination of tyrosine residues on thyroglobulin (organification) and coupling of iodotyrosines to form T3 and T4.
- **PTU vs. Methimazole:**
    - **Mechanism of TPO Inhibition:** Both PTU and methimazole are prodrugs. Methimazole is rapidly converted to its active metabolite, methimazole sulfoxide, which inhibits TPO. PTU is also an active inhibitor of TPO. The primary mechanism for reducing thyroid hormone levels in both drugs is TPO inhibition.
    - **Peripheral Deiodination Inhibition:** PTU, unlike methimazole, also inhibits the Type 1 deiodinase (D1) enzyme. D1 is primarily located in the liver, kidney, and thyroid. Its main function is to convert T4 to the more active T3. Inhibition of D1 reduces the peripheral production of T3, which is crucial in acute hyperthyroidism as T3 mediates many of the severe symptoms. Methimazole does *not* significantly inhibit D1.
    - **Half-life:** Methimazole has a longer half-life (4-6 hours) compared to PTU (1-2 hours), requiring less frequent dosing once steady state is achieved. However, in the acute setting, frequent dosing is often needed regardless.
    - **TSH Receptor Binding:** Neither PTU nor methimazole binds directly to the TSH receptor.
    - **Hepatotoxicity:** PTU carries a higher risk of severe hepatotoxicity (including fulminant hepatic failure) compared to methimazole. Methimazole is generally preferred for long-term management due to this risk.
    - **Renal Excretion:** Neither drug significantly accelerates the renal excretion of preformed thyroid hormones.
- **Rationale for PTU in Thyroid Storm:** In thyroid storm, the rapid reduction of *both* glandular hormone synthesis (via TPO inhibition) and peripheral T3 production (via D1 inhibition) is desired. PTU's ability to inhibit D1 provides an additional, rapid mechanism to lower active T3 levels, making it the preferred agent for the initial management of thyroid storm.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient presents with thyroid storm, a severe hyperthyroid state requiring rapid reduction of thyroid hormone effects.
2. Management includes beta-blockers (to control adrenergic symptoms), iodine (to block hormone release - administered after thionamide), glucocorticoids (to inhibit peripheral T4->T3 conversion and treat potential adrenal insufficiency), and supportive care.
3. An antithyroid drug is needed to block new hormone synthesis. The choice is between PTU and methimazole.
4. The question asks for the specific reason PTU is preferred *in this acute crisis*.
5. Both drugs inhibit TPO, reducing new hormone synthesis.
6. PTU uniquely inhibits Type 1 deiodinase, reducing the conversion of T4 to the more active T3 in peripheral tissues.
7. Lowering peripheral T3 levels rapidly contributes to alleviating the severe symptoms of thyroid storm.
8. Therefore, PTU's dual action (TPO inhibition + D1 inhibition) is the key advantage in the acute setting.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) PTU provides dual action by inhibiting thyroid peroxidase within the gland and inhibiting peripheral conversion of T4 to T3 by Type 1 deiodinase:** This statement accurately describes the key pharmacological difference between PTU and methimazole that makes PTU preferred in thyroid storm. PTU inhibits TPO (like methimazole) and also inhibits D1, reducing peripheral T3 production. This is the correct answer.
- **(B) PTU has a much longer serum half-life than methimazole and requires once-daily dosing:** This is incorrect. Methimazole has a longer half-life (4-6 hours) than PTU (1-2 hours). Both require frequent dosing in the acute setting of thyroid storm.
- **(C) PTU binds directly to the TSH receptor and blocks thyroid-stimulating immunoglobulin binding:** This is incorrect. Neither PTU nor methimazole binds to the TSH receptor. This mechanism is characteristic of certain experimental drugs or potentially antibodies in autoimmune thyroid disease, but not thionamides.
- **(D) PTU carries zero risk of hepatotoxicity compared to methimazole:** This is incorrect. PTU carries a *higher* risk of severe hepatotoxicity than methimazole. This is a major reason why methimazole is preferred for long-term maintenance therapy.
- **(E) PTU accelerates the renal tubular excretion of preformed thyroid hormones:** This is incorrect. Neither PTU nor methimazole significantly affects the renal excretion of preformed thyroid hormones. Strategies to enhance hormone clearance (like hemodialysis or charcoal hemoperfusion) are rarely used and are not related to thionamide pharmacology.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Feature                 | Propylthiouracil (PTU) | Methimazole (MMI) |
|-------------------------|------------------------|--------------------|
| TPO Inhibition          | Yes                    | Yes                |
| Type 1 Deiodinase (D1) Inhibition | Yes                    | No                 |
| Half-life               | 1-2 hours              | 4-6 hours          |
| Hepatotoxicity Risk     | Higher                 | Lower              |
| Teratogenicity          | Risk (1st trimester)   | Risk (1st trimester) |
| Dosing Frequency        | TID-QID                | BID-TID            |
| Preferred in Thyroid Storm | Yes                    | No                 |
| Preferred for Maintenance | No (due to hepatotoxicity) | Yes                |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
In the management of thyroid storm, propylthiouracil (PTU) is preferred over methimazole initially due to its additional ability to inhibit peripheral Type 1 deiodinase, thereby reducing the conversion of T4 to the more active T3 and providing a faster reduction in circulating active thyroid hormone levels. While both drugs inhibit thyroid peroxidase, this unique action of PTU is crucial in the acute crisis.

---

### Question 122: Thyroid Storm Management: Potassium Iodide Timing and Rationale
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The physiological rationale for delaying potassium iodide (KI) administration until at least one hour after initiating antithyroid drug (ATD) therapy (like PTU or methimazole) in the management of thyroid storm, focusing on the mechanism of ATDs and the potential for the Jod-Basedow effect.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old woman with a known history of Graves' disease presents to the emergency department with fever (39.5°C), tachycardia (140 bpm), agitation, and palpitations. Her physical examination reveals a diffusely enlarged, warm, and tender thyroid gland, along with fine tremor and mild exophthalmos. Initial laboratory results show suppressed TSH (<0.01 µIU/mL), markedly elevated free T4 (6.0 ng/dL; normal range 0.8-1.8 ng/dL), and elevated free T3 (1500 pg/mL; normal range 80-200 pg/mL). A diagnosis of thyroid storm is made. The attending physician orders intravenous propylthiouracil (PTU), intravenous propranolol, intravenous hydrocortisone, and a cooling blanket. The physician specifically instructs the nursing staff to hold the administration of a supersaturated potassium iodide (SSKI) solution until at least one hour after the PTU infusion has begun. What is the primary physiological reason for this delay?

(A) Administering iodine before blocking thyroid peroxidase would provide an immediate substrate load, fueling massive synthesis and release of new thyroid hormones (Jod-Basedow effect)
(B) Potassium iodide forms an insoluble chemical precipitate with PTU in the stomach that prevents PTU absorption
(C) Iodide given before PTU permanently destroys the sodium-iodide symporter, causing fatal iodine toxicity
(D) PTU requires 1 hour to neutralize circulating thyroid-binding globulins before iodide can enter the gland
(E) Early iodide administration induces acute metabolic alkalosis that neutralizes the acidic stomach contents

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 45-year-old woman with Graves' disease presenting with classic signs and symptoms of thyroid storm (fever, tachycardia, agitation, palpitations, warm/tender goiter, tremor, exophthalmos).
- **Laboratory Findings:** Suppressed TSH, markedly elevated free T4 and free T3 confirm severe hyperthyroidism consistent with thyroid storm.
- **Treatment Plan:** Standard management for thyroid storm includes ATDs (PTU), beta-blockers (propranolol), glucocorticoids (hydrocortisone), and supportive care (cooling).
- **Specific Instruction:** Delaying SSKI administration by at least 1 hour after starting PTU.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the physiological rationale behind this specific timing instruction.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (甲状腺生理学与病理生理学分子机制推导)
- **Antithyroid Drugs (ATDs):** PTU and methimazole inhibit thyroid hormone synthesis by blocking the enzyme thyroid peroxidase (TPO). TPO is crucial for the iodination of tyrosine residues on thyroglobulin (organification) and the coupling of iodotyrosines to form T3 and T4. Inhibition of TPO prevents the synthesis of new thyroid hormones.
- **Potassium Iodide (KI):** High doses of iodide (like SSKI or Lugol's solution) acutely inhibit thyroid hormone release from the gland (Wolff-Chaikoff effect) and also inhibit TPO-mediated organification, although this effect is less pronounced than the inhibition of release. The primary immediate effect in thyroid storm is the inhibition of hormone release.
- **Jod-Basedow Effect:** This phenomenon occurs when a large amount of iodine is administered to individuals with pre-existing thyroid autonomy (e.g., toxic multinodular goiter, toxic adenoma, or sometimes Graves' disease). The excess iodine provides substrate for the autonomous gland, leading to *increased* synthesis and release of thyroid hormones, worsening hyperthyroidism.
- **Rationale for Delay:** In thyroid storm, the gland is actively synthesizing and releasing excessive hormones. If iodide is given *before* the ATD has sufficiently blocked TPO, the newly supplied iodine substrate can be utilized by the overactive gland, potentially triggering or exacerbating the Jod-Basedow effect, leading to a paradoxical worsening of hyperthyroidism. By waiting at least an hour after starting PTU (which inhibits TPO), the gland's ability to utilize the incoming iodine for new hormone synthesis is significantly reduced, minimizing the risk of the Jod-Basedow effect. The iodide then primarily acts to inhibit hormone release via the Wolff-Chaikoff effect.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient has thyroid storm, characterized by excessive thyroid hormone synthesis and release.
2. Treatment involves blocking hormone synthesis (PTU) and release (iodide).
3. Iodide can potentially increase hormone synthesis if given before synthesis is blocked (Jod-Basedow effect).
4. PTU blocks hormone synthesis by inhibiting TPO.
5. Therefore, PTU must be given first to block TPO before iodide is administered to prevent the Jod-Basedow effect.
6. The delay ensures PTU has time to inhibit TPO before the iodide substrate load is introduced.
7. Option A correctly describes this mechanism (Jod-Basedow effect).

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(B) Potassium iodide forms an insoluble chemical precipitate with PTU in the stomach that prevents PTU absorption:** This is incorrect. There is no known significant chemical interaction between iodide and PTU that prevents PTU absorption. PTU is typically given intravenously in thyroid storm, bypassing gastric absorption issues. Even if given orally, this interaction is not the reason for the delay.
- **(C) Iodide given before PTU permanently destroys the sodium-iodide symporter, causing fatal iodine toxicity:** This is incorrect. Iodide does not permanently destroy the NIS. The NIS is regulated by factors like TSH and iodide levels, and its function can be inhibited acutely (e.g., by high iodide itself - Wolff-Chaikoff effect, or by certain drugs), but not permanently destroyed by this sequence. Iodine toxicity is a concern with very high doses, but the primary reason for the delay is not NIS destruction.
- **(D) PTU requires 1 hour to neutralize circulating thyroid-binding globulins before iodide can enter the gland:** This is incorrect. PTU acts on the thyroid gland to inhibit hormone synthesis. It does not affect circulating thyroid-binding globulins (TBG), which bind thyroid hormones in the plasma. Iodide uptake into the gland is mediated by the NIS and is not dependent on TBG neutralization.
- **(E) Early iodide administration induces acute metabolic alkalosis that neutralizes the acidic stomach contents:** This is incorrect. Iodide administration does not typically cause significant metabolic alkalosis, nor does it have a direct effect on neutralizing stomach acid. The primary effects are on thyroid hormone synthesis and release.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Feature             | Before PTU (Risk)                                  | After PTU (Benefit)                                  |
|----------------------|----------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|
| TPO Activity         | High, actively synthesizing hormones                 | Inhibited by PTU                                   |
| Iodide Effect        | Potential Jod-Basedow effect (increased synthesis) | Primarily Wolff-Chaikoff effect (decreased release) |
| Clinical Outcome     | Worsening hyperthyroidism                            | Inhibition of hormone release, improved control     |
| Timing Rationale     | Avoid substrate load for autonomous synthesis       | Ensure synthesis block before providing substrate   |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
In managing thyroid storm, antithyroid drugs (like PTU or methimazole) must be initiated first to block thyroid hormone synthesis by inhibiting thyroid peroxidase. Potassium iodide (SSKI or Lugol's solution) should then be administered, typically after a delay of at least one hour, to inhibit hormone release via the Wolff-Chaikoff effect. This sequence prevents the potential Jod-Basedow effect, where excess iodine substrate could paradoxically increase hormone synthesis in the presence of an overactive gland.

---

### Question 123: Thyroid Storm Management: Role of High-Dose Glucocorticoids
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The multi-faceted role of high-dose glucocorticoids in the management of thyroid storm, including inhibition of peripheral T4 to T3 conversion, treatment of potential relative adrenal insufficiency, and suppression of autoimmune processes.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old woman with a known history of Graves' disease presents to the emergency department with fever (39.5°C), tachycardia (150 bpm), agitation, and diaphoresis. Her symptoms began acutely 2 days ago after discontinuing her methimazole medication. Physical examination reveals a diffusely enlarged, warm, and tender thyroid gland, bilateral exophthalmos, and a fine tremor. Vital signs show a blood pressure of 140/90 mmHg and respiratory rate of 28 breaths/min. Laboratory results are significant for suppressed TSH (<0.01 µIU/mL), markedly elevated free T4 (5.0 ng/dL; normal range 0.8-1.8 ng/dL), and elevated free T3 (15 pg/mL; normal range 2.3-4.2 pg/mL). A Burch-Wartofsky score is calculated as 45, confirming thyroid storm. Treatment is initiated with intravenous propranolol, propylthiouracil (PTU), and potassium iodide. Intravenous hydrocortisone (100 mg every 8 hours) is also added to the regimen.

Which of the following multi-mechanistic benefits is provided by high-dose systemic glucocorticoid therapy in thyrotoxic crisis?

(A) Inhibits peripheral conversion of T4 to T3, treats potential relative adrenal insufficiency, and suppresses autoimmune thyrocyte hyperactivation
(B) Stimulates the expression of the sodium-iodide symporter to enhance Wolff-Chaikoff blockade
(C) Directly clears thyroid-stimulating immunoglobulins from the circulation via renal filtration
(D) Displaces thyroid hormone from nuclear TR-beta receptors in hepatocytes
(E) Downregulates hypothalamic thyrotropin-releasing hormone without altering pituitary function

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 45-year-old woman with Graves' disease (autoimmune hyperthyroidism).
- **Presentation:** Acute onset of severe hyperthyroid symptoms (fever, tachycardia, agitation, diaphoresis) consistent with thyroid storm.
- **Physical Exam:** Diffuse goiter, exophthalmos (Graves' ophthalmopathy), tremor.
- **Labs:** Suppressed TSH, markedly elevated free T4 and free T3, confirming severe hyperthyroidism. Burch-Wartofsky score >40 confirms thyroid storm.
- **Treatment:** Standard four-drug regimen for thyroid storm initiated (beta-blocker, thionamide, iodide, glucocorticoid).
- **Question Focus:** The specific mechanisms of action of high-dose glucocorticoids in this context.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (甲状腺生理学与病理生理学分子机制推导)
- **Thyroid Storm Pathophysiology:** Exaggerated hyperthyroidism leading to systemic decompensation. High levels of T4 and T3 cause increased metabolic rate, cardiac output, sympathetic activity, and tissue catabolism.
- **Standard Thyroid Storm Treatment:**
    - **Beta-blockers (Propranolol):** Block peripheral effects of catecholamines (tachycardia, tremor, anxiety). Propranolol also inhibits peripheral T4 to T3 conversion (Type 1 deiodinase inhibition).
    - **Thionamides (PTU/Methimazole):** Inhibit thyroid peroxidase (TPO), blocking hormone synthesis and release. PTU also inhibits peripheral T4 to T3 conversion.
    - **Iodide (Potassium Iodide):** Inhibits hormone release (Wolff-Chaikoff effect) and blocks TPO-mediated organification. Given >1 hour after thionamide to prevent iodine from being used for new hormone synthesis.
    - **Glucocorticoids (Hydrocortisone):** Added for multiple reasons.
- **Mechanisms of Glucocorticoids in Thyroid Storm:**
    - **Inhibition of Peripheral T4 to T3 Conversion:** Glucocorticoids inhibit the activity of Type 1 deiodinase (D1), which is primarily responsible for converting T4 to the more active T3 in peripheral tissues like the liver and kidney. This reduces the overall pool of active thyroid hormone.
    - **Treatment of Relative Adrenal Insufficiency:** Severe hyperthyroidism can increase cortisol clearance and potentially lead to relative adrenal insufficiency, especially during stress. Glucocorticoids replace deficient cortisol and support cardiovascular function.
    - **Suppression of Autoimmune Processes:** In Graves' disease, thyroid-stimulating immunoglobulins (TSI) activate the TSH receptor, causing hyperthyroidism. Glucocorticoids have broad immunosuppressive effects and can potentially reduce the production or activity of these autoantibodies, although this effect is slower and less pronounced than the other mechanisms in the acute setting. They also suppress the inflammatory response associated with thyroiditis, if present.
- **Evaluating the Options:**
    - **(A):** Accurately describes three key mechanisms: inhibition of peripheral T4 to T3 conversion (via D1 inhibition), treatment of potential relative adrenal insufficiency, and suppression of autoimmune processes (relevant in Graves').
    - **(B):** Glucocorticoids do not stimulate NIS expression. NIS expression is regulated by TSH and iodide availability. The Wolff-Chaikoff effect is an *inhibition* of hormone synthesis/release due to high iodide, not enhanced by glucocorticoids.
    - **(C):** Glucocorticoids do not directly clear immunoglobulins via renal filtration. Immunoglobulins are large proteins cleared much more slowly, and glucocorticoids primarily modulate immune system function, not renal clearance mechanisms for specific antibodies.
    - **(D):** Glucocorticoids do not directly displace thyroid hormone from nuclear receptors. Thyroid hormone binding to TR is a specific interaction, and glucocorticoids act via their own nuclear receptor (GR).
    - **(E):** Glucocorticoids exert negative feedback on the HPT axis, suppressing both TRH release from the hypothalamus and TSH release from the pituitary. This option only mentions hypothalamic suppression, which is incomplete.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient presents with thyroid storm, a life-threatening exacerbation of hyperthyroidism.
2. The standard treatment includes measures to block hormone synthesis (thionamides, iodide), block peripheral effects (beta-blockers), and reduce active hormone levels.
3. Glucocorticoids are a standard component of this treatment.
4. The question asks for the mechanisms by which glucocorticoids help in thyroid storm.
5. Recall the known effects of glucocorticoids: inhibition of peripheral deiodination (Type 1), support for adrenal function, and immunosuppression.
6. Option (A) encompasses these three key mechanisms relevant to thyroid storm management.
7. Options (B), (C), (D), and (E) describe incorrect or incomplete mechanisms of action for glucocorticoids in this context.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Inhibits peripheral conversion of T4 to T3, treats potential relative adrenal insufficiency, and suppresses autoimmune thyrocyte hyperactivation:** This option correctly identifies three major beneficial effects of glucocorticoids in thyroid storm. Inhibition of Type 1 deiodinase reduces active T3 levels. Severe illness and hyperthyroidism can impair adrenal reserve, and glucocorticoids provide stress-dose support.
